Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I guess what finally got me to come in is… it’s this thing that happens at night, mostly. I’ll be in the kitchen, not really planning anything, just sort of picking at leftovers after everything’s quiet, and I tell myself I’m just having a bite or two. But then it’s like part of me is standing there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” very clear in my head, you know? And another part of me just keeps going. My hand keeps reaching back for more even though I already decided I should stop.

It sounds a little silly saying it out loud, but it doesn’t feel silly when it’s happening. It feels like I’m not quite in charge of it once I’ve started, and that’s what bothers me. I can be sensible about it before, and even while it’s happening I know what I mean to do, but I don’t seem to follow through. That’s what brings me in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.

And the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. But then my hand just goes back again, like I’m not fully in charge of the next bite. I don’t know if I’d call it a compulsion exactly, but it does feel stronger than just wanting food. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\n(none — this is your first inference)\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess what finally got me to come in is… it’s this thing that happens at night, mostly. I’ll be in the kitchen, not really planning anything, just sort of picking at leftovers after everything’s quiet, and I tell myself I’m just having a bite or two. But then it’s like part of me is standing there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” very clear in my head, you know? And another part of me just keeps going. My hand keeps reaching back for more even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nIt sounds a little silly saying it out loud, but it doesn’t feel silly when it’s happening. It feels like I’m not quite in charge of it once I’ve started, and that’s what bothers me. I can be sensible about it before, and even while it’s happening I know what I mean to do, but I don’t seem to follow through. That’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat despite intending to stop, does it feel like you’re acting on a powerful urge or drive that you can’t resist, almost like a compulsion?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. But then it’s like part of me is standing there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” very clear in my head, you know? And another part of me just keeps going. My hand keeps reaching back for more even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nIt sounds a little silly saying it out loud, but it doesn’t feel silly when it’s happening. It feels like I’m not quite in charge of it once I’ve started, and that’s what bothers me. I can be sensible about it before, and even while it’s happening I know what I mean to do, but I don’t seem to follow through. That’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat despite intending to stop, does it feel like you’re acting on a powerful urge or drive that you can’t resist, almost like a compulsion?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. But then my hand just goes back again, like I’m not fully in charge of the next bite. I don’t know if I’d call it a compulsion exactly, but it does feel stronger than just wanting food. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole day falls apart or that I stop doing the things I’m supposed to do. I still get my errands done, I keep appointments, I help around the house, that sort of thing. From the outside, I probably look like I’m managing fine.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel… sluggish, I guess is the word. Not just physically slow, but sort of heavy in myself. And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.

And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.

With the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.

As for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. At least, not in a way that’s obvious to me.

I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, really. I mean, I do blame myself after the eating at night, but that feels pretty tied to that specific thing. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess what finally got me to come in is… it’s this thing that happens at night, mostly. I’ll be in the kitchen, not really planning anything, just sort of picking at leftovers after everything’s quiet, and I tell myself I’m just having a bite or two. But then it’s like part of me is standing there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” very clear in my head, you know? And another part of me just keeps going. My hand keeps reaching back for more even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nIt sounds a little silly saying it out loud, but it doesn’t feel silly when it’s happening. It feels like I’m not quite in charge of it once I’ve started, and that’s what bothers me. I can be sensible about it before, and even while it’s happening I know what I mean to do, but I don’t seem to follow through. That’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat despite intending to stop, does it feel like you’re acting on a powerful urge or drive that you can’t resist, almost like a compulsion?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. But then my hand just goes back again, like I’m not fully in charge of the next bite. I don’t know if I’d call it a compulsion exactly, but it does feel stronger than just wanting food. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole day falls apart or that I stop doing the things I’m supposed to do. I still get my errands done, I keep appointments, I help around the house, that sort of thing. From the outside, I probably look like I’m managing fine.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel… sluggish, I guess is the word. Not just physically slow, but sort of heavy in myself. And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nI don’t really have, like, a particular thought that keeps intruding all day and I have to do some ritual to make it go away, if that’s what you’re asking. And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. No, I don’t think so. I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.70, F42.9, F32.9, F55.80\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess what finally got me to come in is… it’s this thing that happens at night, mostly. I’ll be in the kitchen, not really planning anything, just sort of picking at leftovers after everything’s quiet, and I tell myself I’m just having a bite or two. But then it’s like part of me is standing there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” very clear in my head, you know? And another part of me just keeps going. My hand keeps reaching back for more even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nIt sounds a little silly saying it out loud, but it doesn’t feel silly when it’s happening. It feels like I’m not quite in charge of it once I’ve started, and that’s what bothers me. I can be sensible about it before, and even while it’s happening I know what I mean to do, but I don’t seem to follow through. That’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat despite intending to stop, does it feel like you’re acting on a powerful urge or drive that you can’t resist, almost like a compulsion?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. But then my hand just goes back again, like I’m not fully in charge of the next bite. I don’t know if I’d call it a compulsion exactly, but it does feel stronger than just wanting food. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole day falls apart or that I stop doing the things I’m supposed to do. I still get my errands done, I keep appointments, I help around the house, that sort of thing. From the outside, I probably look like I’m managing fine.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel… sluggish, I guess is the word. Not just physically slow, but sort of heavy in myself. And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nI don’t really have, like, a particular thought that keeps intruding all day and I have to do some ritual to make it go away, if that’s what you’re asking. And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.\n\nAs for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. At least, not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or noticed that it’s harder to focus or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, really. I mean, I do blame myself after the eating at night, but that feels pretty tied to that specific thing. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAs far as focus or decisions… I haven’t paid close attention to it, honestly. I still make the usual decisions I need to make, like what has to get done during the day, errands, household things, that sort of thing. I can get a little preoccupied with thinking, “I shouldn’t have done that,” but I don’t think I’ve had some big change where I can’t concentrate or can’t decide anything. At least nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a time when you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I’d say it causes some problems, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, you know—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, keeping up with the normal day-to-day things. It hasn’t gotten to the point where I’m just not functioning or people have to step in for me.

But the mornings after it happens, I definitely feel it. I feel kind of sluggish, like I’m moving slower and not starting the day the way I want to. And emotionally, I guess I feel less proud of myself, if that makes sense. Like I’m disappointed in how I handled myself the night before. So even if everything still gets done, it sort of colors the day for me, at least for a while.

I do worry about it, probably more than I let on. Not constantly, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. It’s not ruining my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me feel like I’m not managing myself as well as I should be.

That’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat despite intending to stop, does it feel like you’re acting on a powerful urge or drive that you can’t resist, almost like a compulsion?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole day falls apart or that I stop doing the things I’m supposed to do. I still get my errands done, I keep appointments, I help around the house, that sort of thing. I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, I might have a bad evening or wake up feeling kind of discouraged, and then later in the day I’m more or less okay, or at least distracted by regular things. So it doesn’t feel like this constant low mood that just sits there all day for weeks. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this nighttime eating is causing problems in any part of your life, like with your relationships, work, or other activities, or do you worry about it more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it causes some problems, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, you know—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, keeping up with the normal day-to-day things. It hasn’t gotten to the point where I’m just not functioning or people have to step in for me.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I definitely feel it. I feel kind of sluggish, like I’m moving slower and not starting the day the way I want to. And emotionally, I guess I feel less proud of myself, if that makes sense. Like I’m disappointed in how I handled myself the night before. So even if everything still gets done, it sort of colors the day for me, at least for a while.\n\nI do worry about it, probably more than I let on. Not constantly, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. It’s not ruining my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me feel like I’m not managing myself as well as I should be.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you find yourself continuing to eat at night, is it because you're still hungry, or do you sometimes keep eating even when you know you're not really hungry anymore?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Eating_Without_Hunger\nmanifestation: \"A lot of the time I’m not even hungry, because I already had a good dinner with everyone, but later I still find myself opening containers and tasting one thing after another.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A lot of the time, honestly, I don’t think it’s because I’m hungry. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’ll have had a perfectly normal dinner with everyone, a good dinner, enough food, and I won’t be sitting there later with my stomach growling or anything like that.

But then later at night I’ll still end up in the kitchen, opening containers, looking in the fridge, maybe tasting one thing, then another thing, and it sort of keeps going. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need a meal, I’m starving.” It’s more like I start picking at things, and even though I know I’m not really hungry anymore—or maybe wasn’t hungry to begin with—I still keep doing it. That’s what makes it frustrating, because it doesn’t feel like a simple hunger thing.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess what finally got me to come in is… it’s this thing that happens at night, mostly. I’ll be in the kitchen, not really planning anything, just sort of picking at leftovers after everything’s quiet, and I tell myself I’m just having a bite or two. But then it’s like part of me is standing there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” very clear in my head, you know? And another part of me just keeps going. My hand keeps reaching back for more even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nIt sounds a little silly saying it out loud, but it doesn’t feel silly when it’s happening. It feels like I’m not quite in charge of it once I’ve started, and that’s what bothers me. I can be sensible about it before, and even while it’s happening I know what I mean to do, but I don’t seem to follow through. That’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat despite intending to stop, does it feel like you’re acting on a powerful urge or drive that you can’t resist, almost like a compulsion?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. But then my hand just goes back again, like I’m not fully in charge of the next bite. I don’t know if I’d call it a compulsion exactly, but it does feel stronger than just wanting food. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole day falls apart or that I stop doing the things I’m supposed to do. I still get my errands done, I keep appointments, I help around the house, that sort of thing. From the outside, I probably look like I’m managing fine.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel… sluggish, I guess is the word. Not just physically slow, but sort of heavy in myself. And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nI don’t really have, like, a particular thought that keeps intruding all day and I have to do some ritual to make it go away, if that’s what you’re asking. And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.\n\nAs for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAs far as focus or decisions… I haven’t paid close attention to it, honestly. I still make the usual decisions I need to make, like what has to get done during the day, errands, household things, that sort of thing. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’ll have had a perfectly normal dinner with everyone, a good dinner, enough food, and I won’t be sitting there later with my stomach growling or anything like that.\n\nBut then later at night I’ll still end up in the kitchen, opening containers, looking in the fridge, maybe tasting one thing, then another thing, and it sort of keeps going. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need a meal, I’m starving.” It’s more like I start picking at things, and even though I know I’m not really hungry anymore—or maybe wasn’t hungry to begin with—I still keep doing it. That’s what makes it frustrating, because it doesn’t feel like a simple hunger thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're eating at night like this, do you feel like you're no longer in control of how much you eat or whether you stop, even if you want to?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, I might have a bad evening or wake up feeling kind of discouraged, and then later in the day I’m more or less okay, or at least distracted by regular things.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, that’s probably the best way to describe it. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it’s like there’s still a sensible part of me watching it happen and saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more. Just close the container and go sit down.

But then my hand keeps going back anyway. I’ll take another bite, then another little piece, and I tell myself, Okay, this is the last one, and then somehow it isn’t. It’s not like I completely black out or don’t know what I’m doing—I do know—but it feels harder to stop than it should. Like the decision-making part of me is being ignored.

And that’s what makes me feel bad afterward, because in the moment I’m aware enough to know I wanted to stop earlier. I just don’t always seem to follow through.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.70, F42.9, F32.9, F55.80\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess what finally got me to come in is… it’s this thing that happens at night, mostly. I’ll be in the kitchen, not really planning anything, just sort of picking at leftovers after everything’s quiet, and I tell myself I’m just having a bite or two. But then it’s like part of me is standing there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” very clear in my head, you know? And another part of me just keeps going. My hand keeps reaching back for more even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nIt sounds a little silly saying it out loud, but it doesn’t feel silly when it’s happening. It feels like I’m not quite in charge of it once I’ve started, and that’s what bothers me. I can be sensible about it before, and even while it’s happening I know what I mean to do, but I don’t seem to follow through. That’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat despite intending to stop, does it feel like you’re acting on a powerful urge or drive that you can’t resist, almost like a compulsion?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. But then my hand just goes back again, like I’m not fully in charge of the next bite. I don’t know if I’d call it a compulsion exactly, but it does feel stronger than just wanting food. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole day falls apart or that I stop doing the things I’m supposed to do. I still get my errands done, I keep appointments, I help around the house, that sort of thing. From the outside, I probably look like I’m managing fine.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel… sluggish, I guess is the word. Not just physically slow, but sort of heavy in myself. And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nI don’t really have, like, a particular thought that keeps intruding all day and I have to do some ritual to make it go away, if that’s what you’re asking. And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.\n\nAs for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. At least, not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or noticed that it’s harder to focus or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, really. I mean, I do blame myself after the eating at night, but that feels pretty tied to that specific thing. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAs far as focus or decisions… I haven’t paid close attention to it, honestly. I still make the usual decisions I need to make, like what has to get done during the day, errands, household things, that sort of thing. I can get a little preoccupied with thinking, “I shouldn’t have done that,” but I don’t think I’ve had some big change where I can’t concentrate or can’t decide anything. At least nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a time when you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, I might have a bad evening or wake up feeling kind of discouraged, and then later in the day I’m more or less okay, or at least distracted by regular things. So it doesn’t feel like this constant low mood that just sits there all day for weeks. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this nighttime eating is causing problems in any part of your life, like with your relationships, work, or other activities, or do you worry about it more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it causes some problems, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, you know—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, keeping up with the normal day-to-day things. It hasn’t gotten to the point where I’m just not functioning or people have to step in for me.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I definitely feel it. Not really in a big outward way, no. I mean, it hasn’t caused fights with family or anything like that, and I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing work or having trouble with a boss or coworkers. But then my hand just goes back again, like I’m not fully in charge of the next bite. I don’t know if I’d call it a compulsion exactly, but it does feel stronger than just wanting food. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole day falls apart or that I stop doing the things I’m supposed to do. I still get my errands done, I keep appointments, I help around the house, that sort of thing. From the outside, I probably look like I’m managing fine.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel… sluggish, I guess is the word. Not just physically slow, but sort of heavy in myself. And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nI don’t really have, like, a particular thought that keeps intruding all day and I have to do some ritual to make it go away, if that’s what you’re asking. And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.\n\nAs for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. At least, not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or noticed that it’s harder to focus or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, really. I mean, I do blame myself after the eating at night, but that feels pretty tied to that specific thing. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAs far as focus or decisions… I haven’t paid close attention to it, honestly. I still make the usual decisions I need to make, like what has to get done during the day, errands, household things, that sort of thing. I can get a little preoccupied with thinking, “I shouldn’t have done that,” but I don’t think I’ve had some big change where I can’t concentrate or can’t decide anything. At least nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a time when you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, I might have a bad evening or wake up feeling kind of discouraged, and then later in the day I’m more or less okay, or at least distracted by regular things. So it doesn’t feel like this constant low mood that just sits there all day for weeks. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this nighttime eating is causing problems in any part of your life, like with your relationships, work, or other activities, or do you worry about it more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it causes some problems, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, you know—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, keeping up with the normal day-to-day things. It hasn’t gotten to the point where I’m just not functioning or people have to step in for me.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I definitely feel it. I feel kind of sluggish, like I’m moving slower and not starting the day the way I want to. And emotionally, I guess I feel less proud of myself, if that makes sense. Like I’m disappointed in how I handled myself the night before. So even if everything still gets done, it sort of colors the day for me, at least for a while.\n\nI do worry about it, probably more than I let on. Not constantly, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. It’s not ruining my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me feel like I’m not managing myself as well as I should be.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat at night, is it because you're still hungry, or do you sometimes keep eating even when you know you're not really hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, honestly, I don’t think it’s because I’m hungry. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’ll have had a perfectly normal dinner with everyone, a good dinner, enough food, and I won’t be sitting there later with my stomach growling or anything like that.\n\nBut then later at night I’ll still end up in the kitchen, opening containers, looking in the fridge, maybe tasting one thing, then another thing, and it sort of keeps going. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need a meal, I’m starving.” It’s more like I start picking at things, and even though I know I’m not really hungry anymore—or maybe wasn’t hungry to begin with—I still keep doing it. That’s what makes it frustrating, because it doesn’t feel like a simple hunger thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night like this, do you feel like you're no longer in control of how much you eat or whether you stop, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s probably the best way to describe it. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it’s like there’s still a sensible part of me watching it happen and saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more. Just close the container and go sit down.\n\nBut then my hand keeps going back anyway. I’ll take another bite, then another little piece, and I tell myself, Okay, this is the last one, and then somehow it isn’t. It’s not like I completely black out or don’t know what I’m doing—I do know—but it feels harder to stop than it should. Like the decision-making part of me is being ignored.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel bad afterward, because in the moment I’m aware enough to know I wanted to stop earlier. I just don’t always seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever caused problems with your family, friends, or at work, or made you avoid social situations because you were embarrassed or worried about it?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big outward way, no. I mean, it hasn’t caused fights with family or anything like that, and I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing work or having trouble with a boss or coworkers. I’m still doing my normal things—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, doing what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut it does affect me, mostly the next morning. If I’ve been eating like that the night before, I wake up feeling kind of slow and heavy, like I’m not starting the day with the energy I should have. And there’s this feeling of being disappointed in myself, like, Alejandro, you know you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I avoid people because of it, exactly. It’s more private than that. But it does make me feel less proud of myself, and even if nobody else notices, I notice it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly that way, like something has to feel “right.” That sounds a little more… I don’t know, precise or ritual-like than what it feels like to me.

For me it’s more like I get started and then I have trouble getting myself to break away from it. I’ll be standing there with the container open, and part of me is thinking, “All right, put it back now,” but there isn’t really a clear rule in my head like, “I have to eat this many bites,” or “I have to keep going until some feeling clicks into place.” It’s not like I’m trying to make it perfect or complete something, at least not that I’ve noticed.

It’s more of a drifting thing, or getting pulled along. I’m aware I should stop, but I don’t stop as quickly as I meant to. If there’s a loop, it’s probably just that back-and-forth in my head: one more bite, no, stop now, okay just one more. But I can’t say there’s a specific feeling of needing it to be “right.” I haven’t really thought of it that way before.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.70, F42.9, F32.9, F55.80\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess what finally got me to come in is… it’s this thing that happens at night, mostly. I’ll be in the kitchen, not really planning anything, just sort of picking at leftovers after everything’s quiet, and I tell myself I’m just having a bite or two. But then it’s like part of me is standing there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” very clear in my head, you know? And another part of me just keeps going. My hand keeps reaching back for more even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nIt sounds a little silly saying it out loud, but it doesn’t feel silly when it’s happening. It feels like I’m not quite in charge of it once I’ve started, and that’s what bothers me. That’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat despite intending to stop, does it feel like you’re acting on a powerful urge or drive that you can’t resist, almost like a compulsion?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. But then my hand just goes back again, like I’m not fully in charge of the next bite. I don’t know if I’d call it a compulsion exactly, but it does feel stronger than just wanting food. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole day falls apart or that I stop doing the things I’m supposed to do. I still get my errands done, I keep appointments, I help around the house, that sort of thing. From the outside, I probably look like I’m managing fine.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel… sluggish, I guess is the word. Not just physically slow, but sort of heavy in myself. And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nI don’t really have, like, a particular thought that keeps intruding all day and I have to do some ritual to make it go away, if that’s what you’re asking. And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.\n\nAs for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. At least, not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or noticed that it’s harder to focus or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, really. I mean, I do blame myself after the eating at night, but that feels pretty tied to that specific thing. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAs far as focus or decisions… I haven’t paid close attention to it, honestly. I still make the usual decisions I need to make, like what has to get done during the day, errands, household things, that sort of thing. I can get a little preoccupied with thinking, “I shouldn’t have done that,” but I don’t think I’ve had some big change where I can’t concentrate or can’t decide anything. At least nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a time when you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, I might have a bad evening or wake up feeling kind of discouraged, and then later in the day I’m more or less okay, or at least distracted by regular things. So it doesn’t feel like this constant low mood that just sits there all day for weeks. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this nighttime eating is causing problems in any part of your life, like with your relationships, work, or other activities, or do you worry about it more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it causes some problems, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, you know—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, keeping up with the normal day-to-day things. It hasn’t gotten to the point where I’m just not functioning or people have to step in for me.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I definitely feel it. I feel kind of sluggish, like I’m moving slower and not starting the day the way I want to. And emotionally, I guess I feel less proud of myself, if that makes sense. Like I’m disappointed in how I handled myself the night before. So even if everything still gets done, it sort of colors the day for me, at least for a while.\n\nI do worry about it, probably more than I let on. Not constantly, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. It’s not ruining my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me feel like I’m not managing myself as well as I should be.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat at night, is it because you're still hungry, or do you sometimes keep eating even when you know you're not really hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, honestly, I don’t think it’s because I’m hungry. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’ll have had a perfectly normal dinner with everyone, a good dinner, enough food, and I won’t be sitting there later with my stomach growling or anything like that.\n\nBut then later at night I’ll still end up in the kitchen, opening containers, looking in the fridge, maybe tasting one thing, then another thing, and it sort of keeps going. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need a meal, I’m starving.” It’s more like I start picking at things, and even though I know I’m not really hungry anymore—or maybe wasn’t hungry to begin with—I still keep doing it. That’s what makes it frustrating, because it doesn’t feel like a simple hunger thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night like this, do you feel like you're no longer in control of how much you eat or whether you stop, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s probably the best way to describe it. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it’s like there’s still a sensible part of me watching it happen and saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more. Just close the container and go sit down.\n\nBut then my hand keeps going back anyway. I’ll take another bite, then another little piece, and I tell myself, Okay, this is the last one, and then somehow it isn’t. It’s not like I completely black out or don’t know what I’m doing—I do know—but it feels harder to stop than it should. Like the decision-making part of me is being ignored.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel bad afterward, because in the moment I’m aware enough to know I wanted to stop earlier. I just don’t always seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever caused problems with your family, friends, or at work, or made you avoid social situations because you were embarrassed or worried about it?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big outward way, no. I mean, it hasn’t caused fights with family or anything like that, and I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing work or having trouble with a boss or coworkers. I’m still doing my normal things—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, doing what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut it does affect me, mostly the next morning. If I’ve been eating like that the night before, I wake up feeling kind of slow and heavy, like I’m not starting the day with the energy I should have. And there’s this feeling of being disappointed in myself, like, Alejandro, you know you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I avoid people because of it, exactly. It’s more private than that. But it does make me feel less proud of myself, and even if nobody else notices, I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, do you feel like you’re caught in a kind of mental loop or that you have to keep going until something just feels ‘right,’ even if you can’t explain why?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly that way, like something has to feel “right.” That sounds a little more… I don’t know, precise or ritual-like than what it feels like to me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get started and then I have trouble getting myself to break away from it. I’ll be standing there with the container open, and part of me is thinking, “All right, put it back now,” but there isn’t really a clear rule in my head like, “I have to eat this many bites,” or “I have to keep going until some feeling clicks into place.” It’s not like I’m trying to make it perfect or complete something, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more of a drifting thing, or getting pulled along. I’m aware I should stop, but I don’t stop as quickly as I meant to. If there’s a loop, it’s probably just that back-and-forth in my head: one more bite, no, stop now, okay just one more. But I can’t say there’s a specific feeling of needing it to be “right.” I haven’t really thought of it that way before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s not like I completely black out or don’t know what I’m doing—I do know—but it feels harder to stop than it should. Like the decision-making part of me is being ignored.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel bad afterward, because in the moment I’m aware enough to know I wanted to stop earlier. I just don’t always seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever caused problems with your family, friends, or at work, or made you avoid social situations because you were embarrassed or worried about it?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big outward way, no. I mean, it hasn’t caused fights with family or anything like that, and I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing work or having trouble with a boss or coworkers. I’m still doing my normal things—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, doing what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut it does affect me, mostly the next morning. If I’ve been eating like that the night before, I wake up feeling kind of slow and heavy, like I’m not starting the day with the energy I should have. And there’s this feeling of being disappointed in myself, like, Alejandro, you know you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I avoid people because of it, exactly. It’s more private than that. But it does make me feel less proud of myself, and even if nobody else notices, I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, do you feel like you’re caught in a kind of mental loop or that you have to keep going until something just feels ‘right,’ even if you can’t explain why?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly that way, like something has to feel “right.” That sounds a little more… I don’t know, precise or ritual-like than what it feels like to me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get started and then I have trouble getting myself to break away from it. I’ll be standing there with the container open, and part of me is thinking, “All right, put it back now,” but there isn’t really a clear rule in my head like, “I have to eat this many bites,” or “I have to keep going until some feeling clicks into place.” It’s not like I’m trying to make it perfect or complete something, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more of a drifting thing, or getting pulled along. I’m aware I should stop, but I don’t stop as quickly as I meant to. If there’s a loop, it’s probably just that back-and-forth in my head: one more bite, no, stop now, okay just one more. But I can’t say there’s a specific feeling of needing it to be “right.” I haven’t really thought of it that way before.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel so upset or concerned about the nighttime eating that it affects how you feel about yourself or your ability to take care of things during the day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still keeping up with my errands, appointments, and helping around the house, but the mornings after it happens I feel sluggish and less proud of how I’m handling myself.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it does affect how I feel about myself, maybe more than it affects what I actually get done. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart during the day, because I’m not. I still get to my appointments, I still run my errands, I still help around the house and take care of the things I’m supposed to take care of.

But the morning after one of those nights, I can feel it. I’ll wake up kind of sluggish, like I’m carrying the night before with me. And mentally there’s this disappointment, this feeling like, Why did I do that again? I should have handled that better. It makes me feel less proud of myself, I guess.

So it’s not that I can’t function, exactly. I still do what needs doing. But I don’t feel as good about myself while I’m doing it, and I don’t feel as sharp or as ready for the day as I’d like to.
