And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nI don’t really have, like, a particular thought that keeps intruding all day and I have to do some ritual to make it go away, if that’s what you’re asking. And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.\n\nAs for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. At least, not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or noticed that it’s harder to focus or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, really. I mean, I do blame myself after the eating at night, but that feels pretty tied to that specific thing. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAs far as focus or decisions… I haven’t paid close attention to it, honestly. I still make the usual decisions I need to make, like what has to get done during the day, errands, household things, that sort of thing. I can get a little preoccupied with thinking, “I shouldn’t have done that,” but I don’t think I’ve had some big change where I can’t concentrate or can’t decide anything. At least nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a time when you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, I might have a bad evening or wake up feeling kind of discouraged, and then later in the day I’m more or less okay, or at least distracted by regular things. So it doesn’t feel like this constant low mood that just sits there all day for weeks. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this nighttime eating is causing problems in any part of your life, like with your relationships, work, or other activities, or do you worry about it more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it causes some problems, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, you know—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, keeping up with the normal day-to-day things. It hasn’t gotten to the point where I’m just not functioning or people have to step in for me.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I definitely feel it. I feel kind of sluggish, like I’m moving slower and not starting the day the way I want to. And emotionally, I guess I feel less proud of myself, if that makes sense. Like I’m disappointed in how I handled myself the night before. So even if everything still gets done, it sort of colors the day for me, at least for a while.\n\nI do worry about it, probably more than I let on. Not constantly, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. It’s not ruining my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me feel like I’m not managing myself as well as I should be.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat at night, is it because you're still hungry, or do you sometimes keep eating even when you know you're not really hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, honestly, I don’t think it’s because I’m hungry. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’ll have had a perfectly normal dinner with everyone, a good dinner, enough food, and I won’t be sitting there later with my stomach growling or anything like that.\n\nBut then later at night I’ll still end up in the kitchen, opening containers, looking in the fridge, maybe tasting one thing, then another thing, and it sort of keeps going. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need a meal, I’m starving.” It’s more like I start picking at things, and even though I know I’m not really hungry anymore—or maybe wasn’t hungry to begin with—I still keep doing it. That’s what makes it frustrating, because it doesn’t feel like a simple hunger thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night like this, do you feel like you're no longer in control of how much you eat or whether you stop, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s probably the best way to describe it. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it’s like there’s still a sensible part of me watching it happen and saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more. I’ll take another bite, then another little piece, and I tell myself, Okay, this is the last one, and then somehow it isn’t. It’s not like I completely black out or don’t know what I’m doing—I do know—but it feels harder to stop than it should. Like the decision-making part of me is being ignored.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel bad afterward, because in the moment I’m aware enough to know I wanted to stop earlier. I just don’t always seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever caused problems with your family, friends, or at work, or made you avoid social situations because you were embarrassed or worried about it?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big outward way, no. I mean, it hasn’t caused fights with family or anything like that, and I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing work or having trouble with a boss or coworkers. I’m still doing my normal things—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, doing what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut it does affect me, mostly the next morning. If I’ve been eating like that the night before, I wake up feeling kind of slow and heavy, like I’m not starting the day with the energy I should have. And there’s this feeling of being disappointed in myself, like, Alejandro, you know you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I avoid people because of it, exactly. It’s more private than that. But it does make me feel less proud of myself, and even if nobody else notices, I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, do you feel like you’re caught in a kind of mental loop or that you have to keep going until something just feels ‘right,’ even if you can’t explain why?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly that way, like something has to feel “right.” That sounds a little more… I don’t know, precise or ritual-like than what it feels like to me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get started and then I have trouble getting myself to break away from it. I’ll be standing there with the container open, and part of me is thinking, “All right, put it back now,” but there isn’t really a clear rule in my head like, “I have to eat this many bites,” or “I have to keep going until some feeling clicks into place.” It’s not like I’m trying to make it perfect or complete something, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more of a drifting thing, or getting pulled along. I’m aware I should stop, but I don’t stop as quickly as I meant to. If there’s a loop, it’s probably just that back-and-forth in my head: one more bite, no, stop now, okay just one more. But I can’t say there’s a specific feeling of needing it to be “right.” I haven’t really thought of it that way before.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so upset or concerned about the nighttime eating that it affects how you feel about yourself or your ability to take care of things during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does affect how I feel about myself, maybe more than it affects what I actually get done. It makes me feel less proud of myself, I guess.\n\nSo it’s not that I can’t function, exactly. I still do what needs doing. But I don’t feel as good about myself while I’m doing it, and I don’t feel as sharp or as ready for the day as I’d like to.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset or embarrassed that you avoided interacting with others, or felt it seriously interfered with your ability to take care of responsibilities at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding people, no. I mean, I’m not canceling plans or hiding in my room all day because of it. It’s more that it stays with me in this private way the next morning.\n\nLike, I still do what I’m supposed to do. I get to my appointments, I run my errands, I help around the house, I take care of the usual things. So if you were looking from the outside, you might not even notice anything different.\n\nBut I notice it. The mornings after it happens, I feel sluggish, kind of weighed down, and I’m less proud of myself. I’ll be doing the things I need to do, but in my head I’m thinking, you know, I didn’t handle myself the way I wanted to last night. So it doesn’t seriously stop me from taking care of responsibilities, but it does affect how I feel while I’m doing them.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset that it affected your mood or energy for the whole day, or made it hard to focus on tasks you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it affects my mood and my energy, especially in the morning after. I don’t know if I’d say it ruins the whole day, because I do still get through the day. I still go to my appointments if I have them, I still run errands, I still help around the house and do the normal things I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut I can feel the difference. I wake up feeling kind of sluggish, like I’m starting the day from behind, and there’s this disappointed feeling in myself. It’s not even always dramatic, but it’s there — like, “Here we are again, you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.” And then I’m trying to do whatever I need to do that day with that sitting in the back of my mind.\n\nAs far as focus, I can usually still focus enough to get things done, but I don’t feel as sharp or as settled. I might be doing the errand or helping with something at home, but part of me is still thinking about the night before. So it doesn’t completely stop me, but it definitely takes something out of me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, does it feel like you’re caught in a mental loop or that you have an irresistible urge that you can’t resist once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, honestly. It does feel like once it starts, I’m not fully in charge of it the way I want to be.\n\nLike, I’ll go into the kitchen late at night and tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, just a few bites, nothing more. And then there’s this very sensible part of me saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more, close the container, put it away. I can hear that thought pretty clearly.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back. Not like I’m completely unconscious or anything — I know what I’m doing — but it feels like the stopping part of me doesn’t have much strength in that moment. I’ll think, Okay, one more bite, and then another one after that, and it turns into this loop where I keep meaning to stop but I don’t actually stop.\n\nSo yes, I guess I would say it feels like an urge that gets hard to resist once it’s already going. It’s not even that I’m enjoying it the whole time. Part of me is already disappointed while I’m still doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the nighttime eating made you feel so upset that it affected your ability to feel good about yourself for days, or made it hard to engage in your usual routines for more than just the morning after?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it lasts for days in that bigger way, no. I mean, I’m not knocked off my routines for several days afterward or anything like that. I still get up, I still go to my appointments if I have one, I still run the errands I need to run, and I still help around the house. Those things do get done.\n\nBut the morning after, that’s when I really feel it. I’ll wake up feeling sluggish, kind of heavy and slow, and there’s this disappointed feeling with myself. Not like I hate myself for days, but more like… I’m less proud of how I’m handling myself. I’ll be making coffee or getting ready to go somewhere, and I’m thinking, Why did I let it go that far again?\n\nSo it doesn’t really stop my life for more than the next morning, if that makes sense. I keep functioning. But it does color that next part of the day, especially early on. It’s like I’m doing my normal routine, but carrying this little weight of regret with me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not really more than the next morning, no. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it has no effect, because it does. But then it’s like part of me is standing there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” very clear in my head, you know? And another part of me just keeps going. My hand keeps reaching back for more even though I already decided I should stop.\n\nIt sounds a little silly saying it out loud, but it doesn’t feel silly when it’s happening. It feels like I’m not quite in charge of it once I’ve started, and that’s what bothers me. I can be sensible about it before, and even while it’s happening I know what I mean to do, but I don’t seem to follow through. That’s what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat despite intending to stop, does it feel like you’re acting on a powerful urge or drive that you can’t resist, almost like a compulsion?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “I’m going to eat a lot now.” It’s more that once I’ve started picking at the leftovers, especially late at night when the house is quiet, there’s this pull to keep going.\n\nAnd the strange part is I’m not unaware of it. I’m very aware, actually. The sensible part of me is right there saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and I can almost hear myself thinking it clearly. But then my hand just goes back again, like I’m not fully in charge of the next bite. I don’t know if I’d call it a compulsion exactly, but it does feel stronger than just wanting food. It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole day falls apart or that I stop doing the things I’m supposed to do. I still get my errands done, I keep appointments, I help around the house, that sort of thing. From the outside, I probably look like I’m managing fine.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel… sluggish, I guess is the word. Not just physically slow, but sort of heavy in myself. And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nI don’t really have, like, a particular thought that keeps intruding all day and I have to do some ritual to make it go away, if that’s what you’re asking. And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.\n\nAs for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. At least, not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or noticed that it’s harder to focus or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, really. I mean, I do blame myself after the eating at night, but that feels pretty tied to that specific thing. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAs far as focus or decisions… I haven’t paid close attention to it, honestly. I still make the usual decisions I need to make, like what has to get done during the day, errands, household things, that sort of thing. I can get a little preoccupied with thinking, “I shouldn’t have done that,” but I don’t think I’ve had some big change where I can’t concentrate or can’t decide anything. At least nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a time when you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, I might have a bad evening or wake up feeling kind of discouraged, and then later in the day I’m more or less okay, or at least distracted by regular things. So it doesn’t feel like this constant low mood that just sits there all day for weeks. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this nighttime eating is causing problems in any part of your life, like with your relationships, work, or other activities, or do you worry about it more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it causes some problems, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, you know—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, keeping up with the normal day-to-day things. It hasn’t gotten to the point where I’m just not functioning or people have to step in for me.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I definitely feel it. I feel kind of sluggish, like I’m moving slower and not starting the day the way I want to. And emotionally, I guess I feel less proud of myself, if that makes sense. Like I’m disappointed in how I handled myself the night before. So even if everything still gets done, it sort of colors the day for me, at least for a while.\n\nI do worry about it, probably more than I let on. Not constantly, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. It’s not ruining my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me feel like I’m not managing myself as well as I should be.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat at night, is it because you're still hungry, or do you sometimes keep eating even when you know you're not really hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, honestly, I don’t think it’s because I’m hungry. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’ll have had a perfectly normal dinner with everyone, a good dinner, enough food, and I won’t be sitting there later with my stomach growling or anything like that.\n\nBut then later at night I’ll still end up in the kitchen, opening containers, looking in the fridge, maybe tasting one thing, then another thing, and it sort of keeps going. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need a meal, I’m starving.” It’s more like I start picking at things, and even though I know I’m not really hungry anymore—or maybe wasn’t hungry to begin with—I still keep doing it. That’s what makes it frustrating, because it doesn’t feel like a simple hunger thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night like this, do you feel like you're no longer in control of how much you eat or whether you stop, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s probably the best way to describe it. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it’s like there’s still a sensible part of me watching it happen and saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more. Just close the container and go sit down.\n\nBut then my hand keeps going back anyway. I’ll take another bite, then another little piece, and I tell myself, Okay, this is the last one, and then somehow it isn’t. It’s not like I completely black out or don’t know what I’m doing—I do know—but it feels harder to stop than it should. Like the decision-making part of me is being ignored.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel bad afterward, because in the moment I’m aware enough to know I wanted to stop earlier. I just don’t always seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever caused problems with your family, friends, or at work, or made you avoid social situations because you were embarrassed or worried about it?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big outward way, no. I mean, it hasn’t caused fights with family or anything like that, and I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing work or having trouble with a boss or coworkers. I’m still doing my normal things—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, doing what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut it does affect me, mostly the next morning. If I’ve been eating like that the night before, I wake up feeling kind of slow and heavy, like I’m not starting the day with the energy I should have. And there’s this feeling of being disappointed in myself, like, Alejandro, you know you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I avoid people because of it, exactly. It’s more private than that. But it does make me feel less proud of myself, and even if nobody else notices, I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, do you feel like you’re caught in a kind of mental loop or that you have to keep going until something just feels ‘right,’ even if you can’t explain why?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly that way, like something has to feel “right.” That sounds a little more… I don’t know, precise or ritual-like than what it feels like to me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get started and then I have trouble getting myself to break away from it. I’ll be standing there with the container open, and part of me is thinking, “All right, put it back now,” but there isn’t really a clear rule in my head like, “I have to eat this many bites,” or “I have to keep going until some feeling clicks into place.” It’s not like I’m trying to make it perfect or complete something, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more of a drifting thing, or getting pulled along. I’m aware I should stop, but I don’t stop as quickly as I meant to. If there’s a loop, it’s probably just that back-and-forth in my head: one more bite, no, stop now, okay just one more. But I can’t say there’s a specific feeling of needing it to be “right.” I haven’t really thought of it that way before.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so upset or concerned about the nighttime eating that it affects how you feel about yourself or your ability to take care of things during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does affect how I feel about myself, maybe more than it affects what I actually get done. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart during the day, because I’m not. I still get to my appointments, I still run my errands, I still help around the house and take care of the things I’m supposed to take care of.\n\nBut the morning after one of those nights, I can feel it. I’ll wake up kind of sluggish, like I’m carrying the night before with me. And mentally there’s this disappointment, this feeling like, Why did I do that again? I should have handled that better. It makes me feel less proud of myself, I guess.\n\nSo it’s not that I can’t function, exactly. I still do what needs doing. But I don’t feel as good about myself while I’m doing it, and I don’t feel as sharp or as ready for the day as I’d like to.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset or embarrassed that you avoided interacting with others, or felt it seriously interfered with your ability to take care of responsibilities at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding people, no. I mean, I’m not canceling plans or hiding in my room all day because of it. It’s more that it stays with me in this private way the next morning.\n\nLike, I still do what I’m supposed to do. I get to my appointments, I run my errands, I help around the house, I take care of the usual things. So if you were looking from the outside, you might not even notice anything different.\n\nBut I notice it. The mornings after it happens, I feel sluggish, kind of weighed down, and I’m less proud of myself. I’ll be doing the things I need to do, but in my head I’m thinking, you know, I didn’t handle myself the way I wanted to last night. So it doesn’t seriously stop me from taking care of responsibilities, but it does affect how I feel while I’m doing them.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset that it affected your mood or energy for the whole day, or made it hard to focus on tasks you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it affects my mood and my energy, especially in the morning after. I don’t know if I’d say it ruins the whole day, because I do still get through the day. I still go to my appointments if I have them, I still run errands, I still help around the house and do the normal things I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut I can feel the difference. I wake up feeling kind of sluggish, like I’m starting the day from behind, and there’s this disappointed feeling in myself. It’s not even always dramatic, but it’s there — like, “Here we are again, you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.” And then I’m trying to do whatever I need to do that day with that sitting in the back of my mind.\n\nAs far as focus, I can usually still focus enough to get things done, but I don’t feel as sharp or as settled. I might be doing the errand or helping with something at home, but part of me is still thinking about the night before. So it doesn’t completely stop me, but it definitely takes something out of me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, does it feel like you’re caught in a mental loop or that you have an irresistible urge that you can’t resist once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, honestly. It does feel like once it starts, I’m not fully in charge of it the way I want to be.\n\nLike, I’ll go into the kitchen late at night and tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, just a few bites, nothing more. And then there’s this very sensible part of me saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more, close the container, put it away. I can hear that thought pretty clearly.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back. Not like I’m completely unconscious or anything — I know what I’m doing — but it feels like the stopping part of me doesn’t have much strength in that moment. I’ll think, Okay, one more bite, and then another one after that, and it turns into this loop where I keep meaning to stop but I don’t actually stop.\n\nSo yes, I guess I would say it feels like an urge that gets hard to resist once it’s already going. It’s not even that I’m enjoying it the whole time. Part of me is already disappointed while I’m still doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the nighttime eating made you feel so upset that it affected your ability to feel good about yourself for days, or made it hard to engage in your usual routines for more than just the morning after?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it lasts for days in that bigger way, no. I mean, I’m not knocked off my routines for several days afterward or anything like that. I still get up, I still go to my appointments if I have one, I still run the errands I need to run, and I still help around the house. Those things do get done.\n\nBut the morning after, that’s when I really feel it. I’ll wake up feeling sluggish, kind of heavy and slow, and there’s this disappointed feeling with myself. Not like I hate myself for days, but more like… I’m less proud of how I’m handling myself. I’ll be making coffee or getting ready to go somewhere, and I’m thinking, Why did I let it go that far again?\n\nSo it doesn’t really stop my life for more than the next morning, if that makes sense. I keep functioning. But it does color that next part of the day, especially early on. It’s like I’m doing my normal routine, but carrying this little weight of regret with me.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when the nighttime eating upset you so much that it affected your ability to feel like yourself or engage in your usual routines for more than just the next morning?\n\nPatient: Not really more than the next morning, no. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it has no effect, because it does. The morning after, I can feel pretty sluggish, like my body is moving slower than it should, and I’m not exactly feeling proud of myself. There’s that feeling of, “Why did I do that again?” and it kind of sits with me while I’m getting started for the day.\n\nBut as far as my usual routines, I’m still doing them. I still keep my appointments if I have them, I still go run errands, I still help around the house with the things I’m supposed to help with. I’m not staying in bed all day or disappearing from everything.\n\nIt’s more like I’m doing those things with less energy and with that disappointed feeling in the background. So it affects me, yes, but I wouldn’t say it takes me out of my life for days or stops me from being myself beyond that next morning.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s like I’m doing my normal routine, but carrying this little weight of regret with me.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when the nighttime eating upset you so much that it affected your ability to feel like yourself or engage in your usual routines for more than just the next morning?\n\nPatient: Not really more than the next morning, no. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it has no effect, because it does. The morning after, I can feel pretty sluggish, like my body is moving slower than it should, and I’m not exactly feeling proud of myself. There’s that feeling of, “Why did I do that again?” and it kind of sits with me while I’m getting started for the day.\n\nBut as far as my usual routines, I’m still doing them. I still keep my appointments if I have them, I still go run errands, I still help around the house with the things I’m supposed to help with. I’m not staying in bed all day or disappearing from everything.\n\nIt’s more like I’m doing those things with less energy and with that disappointed feeling in the background.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really beyond the morning after, no. I mean, it definitely bothers me, and I don’t want to make it sound like I just shrug it off, because I don’t. The next morning I usually feel sluggish, kind of slow and heavy, and there’s this disappointed feeling in the background, like, Alejandro, you said you weren’t going to do that again, and then you did.

But I still function. I still get up and do the things I’m supposed to do. If I have errands, I go do them. If I have an appointment, I keep it. Around the house, I still help with what needs doing. It’s not like I’m unable to manage the day or I cancel everything because of it.

It’s more that I’m going through the normal routine feeling a little off, and not very proud of how I handled myself the night before. By later on, I’m usually moving along okay. So yes, it affects my mood some the next morning, but I wouldn’t say it carries on and stops me from functioning normally after that.

At least nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a time when you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’ll have had a perfectly normal dinner with everyone, a good dinner, enough food, and I won’t be sitting there later with my stomach growling or anything like that.\n\nBut then later at night I’ll still end up in the kitchen, opening containers, looking in the fridge, maybe tasting one thing, then another thing, and it sort of keeps going. I still get to my appointments, I still run my errands, I still help around the house and take care of the things I’m supposed to take care of.\n\nBut the morning after one of those nights, I can feel it. I’ll wake up kind of sluggish, like I’m carrying the night before with me. And mentally there’s this disappointment, this feeling like, Why did I do that again? I should have handled that better. It makes me feel less proud of myself, I guess.\n\nSo it’s not that I can’t function, exactly. I still do what needs doing. But I don’t feel as good about myself while I’m doing it, and I don’t feel as sharp or as ready for the day as I’d like to.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset or embarrassed that you avoided interacting with others, or felt it seriously interfered with your ability to take care of responsibilities at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding people, no. I mean, I’m not canceling plans or hiding in my room all day because of it. It’s more that it stays with me in this private way the next morning.\n\nLike, I still do what I’m supposed to do. I get to my appointments, I run my errands, I help around the house, I take care of the usual things. So if you were looking from the outside, you might not even notice anything different.\n\nBut I notice it. The mornings after it happens, I feel sluggish, kind of weighed down, and I’m less proud of myself. I’ll be doing the things I need to do, but in my head I’m thinking, you know, I didn’t handle myself the way I wanted to last night. So it doesn’t seriously stop me from taking care of responsibilities, but it does affect how I feel while I’m doing them.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset that it affected your mood or energy for the whole day, or made it hard to focus on tasks you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it affects my mood and my energy, especially in the morning after. I wake up feeling kind of sluggish, like I’m starting the day from behind, and there’s this disappointed feeling in myself. It does feel like once it starts, I’m not fully in charge of it the way I want to be.\n\nLike, I’ll go into the kitchen late at night and tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, just a few bites, nothing more. It’s not even that I’m enjoying it the whole time. Part of me is already disappointed while I’m still doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the nighttime eating made you feel so upset that it affected your ability to feel good about yourself for days, or made it hard to engage in your usual routines for more than just the morning after?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it lasts for days in that bigger way, no. It’s like I’m doing my normal routine, but carrying this little weight of regret with me.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when the nighttime eating upset you so much that it affected your ability to feel like yourself or engage in your usual routines for more than just the next morning?\n\nPatient: Not really more than the next morning, no. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.\n\nAs for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. At least, not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or noticed that it’s harder to focus or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, really. I mean, I do blame myself after the eating at night, but that feels pretty tied to that specific thing. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAs far as focus or decisions… I haven’t paid close attention to it, honestly. I still make the usual decisions I need to make, like what has to get done during the day, errands, household things, that sort of thing. I can get a little preoccupied with thinking, “I shouldn’t have done that,” but I don’t think I’ve had some big change where I can’t concentrate or can’t decide anything. At least nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a time when you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, I might have a bad evening or wake up feeling kind of discouraged, and then later in the day I’m more or less okay, or at least distracted by regular things. So it doesn’t feel like this constant low mood that just sits there all day for weeks. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this nighttime eating is causing problems in any part of your life, like with your relationships, work, or other activities, or do you worry about it more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it causes some problems, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, you know—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, keeping up with the normal day-to-day things. It hasn’t gotten to the point where I’m just not functioning or people have to step in for me.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I definitely feel it. I feel kind of sluggish, like I’m moving slower and not starting the day the way I want to. And emotionally, I guess I feel less proud of myself, if that makes sense. Like I’m disappointed in how I handled myself the night before. So even if everything still gets done, it sort of colors the day for me, at least for a while.\n\nI do worry about it, probably more than I let on. Not constantly, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. It’s not ruining my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me feel like I’m not managing myself as well as I should be.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat at night, is it because you're still hungry, or do you sometimes keep eating even when you know you're not really hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, honestly, I don’t think it’s because I’m hungry. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’ll have had a perfectly normal dinner with everyone, a good dinner, enough food, and I won’t be sitting there later with my stomach growling or anything like that.\n\nBut then later at night I’ll still end up in the kitchen, opening containers, looking in the fridge, maybe tasting one thing, then another thing, and it sort of keeps going. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need a meal, I’m starving.” It’s more like I start picking at things, and even though I know I’m not really hungry anymore—or maybe wasn’t hungry to begin with—I still keep doing it. That’s what makes it frustrating, because it doesn’t feel like a simple hunger thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night like this, do you feel like you're no longer in control of how much you eat or whether you stop, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s probably the best way to describe it. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it’s like there’s still a sensible part of me watching it happen and saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more. Just close the container and go sit down.\n\nBut then my hand keeps going back anyway. I’ll take another bite, then another little piece, and I tell myself, Okay, this is the last one, and then somehow it isn’t. It’s not like I completely black out or don’t know what I’m doing—I do know—but it feels harder to stop than it should. Like the decision-making part of me is being ignored.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel bad afterward, because in the moment I’m aware enough to know I wanted to stop earlier. I just don’t always seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever caused problems with your family, friends, or at work, or made you avoid social situations because you were embarrassed or worried about it?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big outward way, no. I mean, it hasn’t caused fights with family or anything like that, and I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing work or having trouble with a boss or coworkers. I’m still doing my normal things—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, doing what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut it does affect me, mostly the next morning. If I’ve been eating like that the night before, I wake up feeling kind of slow and heavy, like I’m not starting the day with the energy I should have. And there’s this feeling of being disappointed in myself, like, Alejandro, you know you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I avoid people because of it, exactly. It’s more private than that. But it does make me feel less proud of myself, and even if nobody else notices, I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, do you feel like you’re caught in a kind of mental loop or that you have to keep going until something just feels ‘right,’ even if you can’t explain why?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly that way, like something has to feel “right.” That sounds a little more… I don’t know, precise or ritual-like than what it feels like to me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get started and then I have trouble getting myself to break away from it. I’ll be standing there with the container open, and part of me is thinking, “All right, put it back now,” but there isn’t really a clear rule in my head like, “I have to eat this many bites,” or “I have to keep going until some feeling clicks into place.” It’s not like I’m trying to make it perfect or complete something, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more of a drifting thing, or getting pulled along. I’m aware I should stop, but I don’t stop as quickly as I meant to. If there’s a loop, it’s probably just that back-and-forth in my head: one more bite, no, stop now, okay just one more. But I can’t say there’s a specific feeling of needing it to be “right.” I haven’t really thought of it that way before.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so upset or concerned about the nighttime eating that it affects how you feel about yourself or your ability to take care of things during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does affect how I feel about myself, maybe more than it affects what I actually get done. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart during the day, because I’m not. I still get to my appointments, I still run my errands, I still help around the house and take care of the things I’m supposed to take care of.\n\nBut the morning after one of those nights, I can feel it. I’ll wake up kind of sluggish, like I’m carrying the night before with me. And mentally there’s this disappointment, this feeling like, Why did I do that again? I should have handled that better. It makes me feel less proud of myself, I guess.\n\nSo it’s not that I can’t function, exactly. I still do what needs doing. But I don’t feel as good about myself while I’m doing it, and I don’t feel as sharp or as ready for the day as I’d like to.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset or embarrassed that you avoided interacting with others, or felt it seriously interfered with your ability to take care of responsibilities at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding people, no. I mean, I’m not canceling plans or hiding in my room all day because of it. It’s more that it stays with me in this private way the next morning.\n\nLike, I still do what I’m supposed to do. I get to my appointments, I run my errands, I help around the house, I take care of the usual things. So if you were looking from the outside, you might not even notice anything different.\n\nBut I notice it. The mornings after it happens, I feel sluggish, kind of weighed down, and I’m less proud of myself. I’ll be doing the things I need to do, but in my head I’m thinking, you know, I didn’t handle myself the way I wanted to last night. So it doesn’t seriously stop me from taking care of responsibilities, but it does affect how I feel while I’m doing them.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset that it affected your mood or energy for the whole day, or made it hard to focus on tasks you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it affects my mood and my energy, especially in the morning after. I don’t know if I’d say it ruins the whole day, because I do still get through the day. I still go to my appointments if I have them, I still run errands, I still help around the house and do the normal things I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut I can feel the difference. I wake up feeling kind of sluggish, like I’m starting the day from behind, and there’s this disappointed feeling in myself. It’s not even always dramatic, but it’s there — like, “Here we are again, you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.” And then I’m trying to do whatever I need to do that day with that sitting in the back of my mind.\n\nAs far as focus, I can usually still focus enough to get things done, but I don’t feel as sharp or as settled. I might be doing the errand or helping with something at home, but part of me is still thinking about the night before. So it doesn’t completely stop me, but it definitely takes something out of me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, does it feel like you’re caught in a mental loop or that you have an irresistible urge that you can’t resist once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, honestly. It does feel like once it starts, I’m not fully in charge of it the way I want to be.\n\nLike, I’ll go into the kitchen late at night and tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, just a few bites, nothing more. And then there’s this very sensible part of me saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more, close the container, put it away. I can hear that thought pretty clearly.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back. Not like I’m completely unconscious or anything — I know what I’m doing — but it feels like the stopping part of me doesn’t have much strength in that moment. I’ll think, Okay, one more bite, and then another one after that, and it turns into this loop where I keep meaning to stop but I don’t actually stop.\n\nSo yes, I guess I would say it feels like an urge that gets hard to resist once it’s already going. It’s not even that I’m enjoying it the whole time. Part of me is already disappointed while I’m still doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the nighttime eating made you feel so upset that it affected your ability to feel good about yourself for days, or made it hard to engage in your usual routines for more than just the morning after?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it lasts for days in that bigger way, no. I mean, I’m not knocked off my routines for several days afterward or anything like that. I still get up, I still go to my appointments if I have one, I still run the errands I need to run, and I still help around the house. Those things do get done.\n\nBut the morning after, that’s when I really feel it. I’ll wake up feeling sluggish, kind of heavy and slow, and there’s this disappointed feeling with myself. Not like I hate myself for days, but more like… I’m less proud of how I’m handling myself. I’ll be making coffee or getting ready to go somewhere, and I’m thinking, Why did I let it go that far again?\n\nSo it doesn’t really stop my life for more than the next morning, if that makes sense. I keep functioning. But it does color that next part of the day, especially early on. It’s like I’m doing my normal routine, but carrying this little weight of regret with me.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when the nighttime eating upset you so much that it affected your ability to feel like yourself or engage in your usual routines for more than just the next morning?\n\nPatient: Not really more than the next morning, no. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it has no effect, because it does. The morning after, I can feel pretty sluggish, like my body is moving slower than it should, and I’m not exactly feeling proud of myself. There’s that feeling of, “Why did I do that again?” and it kind of sits with me while I’m getting started for the day.\n\nBut as far as my usual routines, I’m still doing them. I still keep my appointments if I have them, I still go run errands, I still help around the house with the things I’m supposed to help with. I’m not staying in bed all day or disappearing from everything.\n\nIt’s more like I’m doing those things with less energy and with that disappointed feeling in the background. So it affects me, yes, but I wouldn’t say it takes me out of my life for days or stops me from being myself beyond that next morning.\n\nDoctor: Has the nighttime eating ever upset you so much that it affected your mood or ability to function normally for more than just the morning after?\n\nPatient: Not really beyond the morning after, no. I mean, it definitely bothers me, and I don’t want to make it sound like I just shrug it off, because I don’t. The next morning I usually feel sluggish, kind of slow and heavy, and there’s this disappointed feeling in the background, like, Alejandro, you said you weren’t going to do that again, and then you did.\n\nBut I still function. I still get up and do the things I’m supposed to do. If I have errands, I go do them. If I have an appointment, I keep it. Around the house, I still help with what needs doing. It’s not like I’m unable to manage the day or I cancel everything because of it.\n\nIt’s more that I’m going through the normal routine feeling a little off, and not very proud of how I handled myself the night before. By later on, I’m usually moving along okay. So yes, it affects my mood some the next morning, but I wouldn’t say it carries on and stops me from functioning normally after that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, “completely unable” is a strong way to put it, but it does feel like something close to that in the moment. It’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’m aware. I’ll be standing there at night, picking at the leftovers, and the sensible part of me is saying, That’s enough, Alejandro. Close the container, put it away, go sit down.

But then my hand just keeps going back. One more bite, then another little piece, then I’m telling myself, Well, I already started, or I’ll stop after this. And I don’t stop when I meant to. That’s the part that bothers me, because it feels like my mind has made the decision to stop, but my body is still reaching for it anyway.

So I wouldn’t say I’m blacked out or anything like that. I know it’s happening. But the control feels… weakened, I guess. Like I’m watching myself ignore the better judgment I already had.

It feels like I mean to stop and then I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like how you feel about yourself or how you function during the day?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. From the outside, I probably look like I’m managing fine.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I feel… sluggish, I guess is the word. Not just physically slow, but sort of heavy in myself. And I feel disappointed, like, “Why did you do that again?” It affects how I feel about myself more than it affects my schedule, if that makes sense. I’m still functioning, but I don’t feel very proud of how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that keep coming back, even when you don’t want them, or found yourself doing something over and over again because you feel you have to, even if it doesn’t make sense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I do have thoughts about the eating that come back, like being annoyed with myself afterward, or telling myself I shouldn’t let it happen again, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean.\n\nI don’t really have, like, a particular thought that keeps intruding all day and I have to do some ritual to make it go away, if that’s what you’re asking. And I’m not someone who has to check the door ten times or wash my hands over and over or anything like that. At least, nothing that stands out to me.\n\nWith the eating, it’s more… once I’m in the moment, I keep going even though I meant to stop. It doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of some rule in my head or because something terrible will happen if I don’t. It’s more of a pull, I guess. But outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself doing repeated things because I feel I have to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly, no. I mean… I have moments where I feel pretty disappointed in myself, especially after the nighttime eating, but outside of that, I don’t know that I’ve noticed myself feeling hopeless exactly.\n\nAs for losing interest in things, I’d have to think about that. I still do the things I normally do, and I don’t think I’ve been avoiding them because I don’t care anymore. It’s more that this one issue kind of hangs over me afterward, and then I get into my head about it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly felt down all the time or stopped enjoying life in general. At least, not in a way that’s obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or noticed that it’s harder to focus or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, really. I mean, I do blame myself after the eating at night, but that feels pretty tied to that specific thing. It’s not like I’m walking around all day feeling guilty about everything or thinking I’ve ruined things for everyone, if that’s what you mean.\n\nAs far as focus or decisions… I haven’t paid close attention to it, honestly. I still make the usual decisions I need to make, like what has to get done during the day, errands, household things, that sort of thing. I can get a little preoccupied with thinking, “I shouldn’t have done that,” but I don’t think I’ve had some big change where I can’t concentrate or can’t decide anything. At least nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a time when you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had low moments, sure, especially when I’m upset with myself, but I can’t remember a stretch where I was down most of the day, every day, for two weeks straight.\n\nI mean, I might have a bad evening or wake up feeling kind of discouraged, and then later in the day I’m more or less okay, or at least distracted by regular things. So it doesn’t feel like this constant low mood that just sits there all day for weeks. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like this nighttime eating is causing problems in any part of your life, like with your relationships, work, or other activities, or do you worry about it more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it causes some problems, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do, you know—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, keeping up with the normal day-to-day things. It hasn’t gotten to the point where I’m just not functioning or people have to step in for me.\n\nBut the mornings after it happens, I definitely feel it. I feel kind of sluggish, like I’m moving slower and not starting the day the way I want to. And emotionally, I guess I feel less proud of myself, if that makes sense. Like I’m disappointed in how I handled myself the night before. So even if everything still gets done, it sort of colors the day for me, at least for a while.\n\nI do worry about it, probably more than I let on. Not constantly, but enough that it sits in the back of my mind. It’s not ruining my relationships or anything like that, but it does make me feel like I’m not managing myself as well as I should be.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself continuing to eat at night, is it because you're still hungry, or do you sometimes keep eating even when you know you're not really hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, honestly, I don’t think it’s because I’m hungry. That’s the part that bothers me, because I’ll have had a perfectly normal dinner with everyone, a good dinner, enough food, and I won’t be sitting there later with my stomach growling or anything like that.\n\nBut then later at night I’ll still end up in the kitchen, opening containers, looking in the fridge, maybe tasting one thing, then another thing, and it sort of keeps going. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need a meal, I’m starving.” It’s more like I start picking at things, and even though I know I’m not really hungry anymore—or maybe wasn’t hungry to begin with—I still keep doing it. That’s what makes it frustrating, because it doesn’t feel like a simple hunger thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night like this, do you feel like you're no longer in control of how much you eat or whether you stop, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s probably the best way to describe it. Once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, it’s like there’s still a sensible part of me watching it happen and saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more. Just close the container and go sit down.\n\nBut then my hand keeps going back anyway. I’ll take another bite, then another little piece, and I tell myself, Okay, this is the last one, and then somehow it isn’t. It’s not like I completely black out or don’t know what I’m doing—I do know—but it feels harder to stop than it should. Like the decision-making part of me is being ignored.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel bad afterward, because in the moment I’m aware enough to know I wanted to stop earlier. I just don’t always seem to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever caused problems with your family, friends, or at work, or made you avoid social situations because you were embarrassed or worried about it?\n\nPatient: Not really in a big outward way, no. I mean, it hasn’t caused fights with family or anything like that, and I’m not working now, so it’s not like I’m missing work or having trouble with a boss or coworkers. I’m still doing my normal things—getting to appointments, running errands, helping around the house, doing what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut it does affect me, mostly the next morning. If I’ve been eating like that the night before, I wake up feeling kind of slow and heavy, like I’m not starting the day with the energy I should have. And there’s this feeling of being disappointed in myself, like, Alejandro, you know you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I avoid people because of it, exactly. It’s more private than that. But it does make me feel less proud of myself, and even if nobody else notices, I notice it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, do you feel like you’re caught in a kind of mental loop or that you have to keep going until something just feels ‘right,’ even if you can’t explain why?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly that way, like something has to feel “right.” That sounds a little more… I don’t know, precise or ritual-like than what it feels like to me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I get started and then I have trouble getting myself to break away from it. I’ll be standing there with the container open, and part of me is thinking, “All right, put it back now,” but there isn’t really a clear rule in my head like, “I have to eat this many bites,” or “I have to keep going until some feeling clicks into place.” It’s not like I’m trying to make it perfect or complete something, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more of a drifting thing, or getting pulled along. I’m aware I should stop, but I don’t stop as quickly as I meant to. If there’s a loop, it’s probably just that back-and-forth in my head: one more bite, no, stop now, okay just one more. But I can’t say there’s a specific feeling of needing it to be “right.” I haven’t really thought of it that way before.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so upset or concerned about the nighttime eating that it affects how you feel about yourself or your ability to take care of things during the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does affect how I feel about myself, maybe more than it affects what I actually get done. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart during the day, because I’m not. I still get to my appointments, I still run my errands, I still help around the house and take care of the things I’m supposed to take care of.\n\nBut the morning after one of those nights, I can feel it. I’ll wake up kind of sluggish, like I’m carrying the night before with me. And mentally there’s this disappointment, this feeling like, Why did I do that again? I should have handled that better. It makes me feel less proud of myself, I guess.\n\nSo it’s not that I can’t function, exactly. I still do what needs doing. But I don’t feel as good about myself while I’m doing it, and I don’t feel as sharp or as ready for the day as I’d like to.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset or embarrassed that you avoided interacting with others, or felt it seriously interfered with your ability to take care of responsibilities at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s gotten to the point where I’m avoiding people, no. I mean, I’m not canceling plans or hiding in my room all day because of it. It’s more that it stays with me in this private way the next morning.\n\nLike, I still do what I’m supposed to do. I get to my appointments, I run my errands, I help around the house, I take care of the usual things. So if you were looking from the outside, you might not even notice anything different.\n\nBut I notice it. The mornings after it happens, I feel sluggish, kind of weighed down, and I’m less proud of myself. I’ll be doing the things I need to do, but in my head I’m thinking, you know, I didn’t handle myself the way I wanted to last night. So it doesn’t seriously stop me from taking care of responsibilities, but it does affect how I feel while I’m doing them.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset that it affected your mood or energy for the whole day, or made it hard to focus on tasks you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it affects my mood and my energy, especially in the morning after. I don’t know if I’d say it ruins the whole day, because I do still get through the day. I still go to my appointments if I have them, I still run errands, I still help around the house and do the normal things I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut I can feel the difference. I wake up feeling kind of sluggish, like I’m starting the day from behind, and there’s this disappointed feeling in myself. It’s not even always dramatic, but it’s there — like, “Here we are again, you didn’t handle that the way you wanted to.” And then I’m trying to do whatever I need to do that day with that sitting in the back of my mind.\n\nAs far as focus, I can usually still focus enough to get things done, but I don’t feel as sharp or as settled. I might be doing the errand or helping with something at home, but part of me is still thinking about the night before. So it doesn’t completely stop me, but it definitely takes something out of me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in the kitchen at night and you keep eating even though you meant to stop, does it feel like you’re caught in a mental loop or that you have an irresistible urge that you can’t resist once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the part that bothers me the most, honestly. It does feel like once it starts, I’m not fully in charge of it the way I want to be.\n\nLike, I’ll go into the kitchen late at night and tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, just a few bites, nothing more. And then there’s this very sensible part of me saying, That’s enough, Alejandro, you don’t need any more, close the container, put it away. I can hear that thought pretty clearly.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back. Not like I’m completely unconscious or anything — I know what I’m doing — but it feels like the stopping part of me doesn’t have much strength in that moment. I’ll think, Okay, one more bite, and then another one after that, and it turns into this loop where I keep meaning to stop but I don’t actually stop.\n\nSo yes, I guess I would say it feels like an urge that gets hard to resist once it’s already going. It’s not even that I’m enjoying it the whole time. Part of me is already disappointed while I’m still doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the nighttime eating made you feel so upset that it affected your ability to feel good about yourself for days, or made it hard to engage in your usual routines for more than just the morning after?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it lasts for days in that bigger way, no. I mean, I’m not knocked off my routines for several days afterward or anything like that. I still get up, I still go to my appointments if I have one, I still run the errands I need to run, and I still help around the house. Those things do get done.\n\nBut the morning after, that’s when I really feel it. I’ll wake up feeling sluggish, kind of heavy and slow, and there’s this disappointed feeling with myself. Not like I hate myself for days, but more like… I’m less proud of how I’m handling myself. I’ll be making coffee or getting ready to go somewhere, and I’m thinking, Why did I let it go that far again?\n\nSo it doesn’t really stop my life for more than the next morning, if that makes sense. I keep functioning. But it does color that next part of the day, especially early on. It’s like I’m doing my normal routine, but carrying this little weight of regret with me.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when the nighttime eating upset you so much that it affected your ability to feel like yourself or engage in your usual routines for more than just the next morning?\n\nPatient: Not really more than the next morning, no. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it has no effect, because it does. The morning after, I can feel pretty sluggish, like my body is moving slower than it should, and I’m not exactly feeling proud of myself. There’s that feeling of, “Why did I do that again?” and it kind of sits with me while I’m getting started for the day.\n\nBut as far as my usual routines, I’m still doing them. I still keep my appointments if I have them, I still go run errands, I still help around the house with the things I’m supposed to help with. I’m not staying in bed all day or disappearing from everything.\n\nIt’s more like I’m doing those things with less energy and with that disappointed feeling in the background. So it affects me, yes, but I wouldn’t say it takes me out of my life for days or stops me from being myself beyond that next morning.\n\nDoctor: Has the nighttime eating ever upset you so much that it affected your mood or ability to function normally for more than just the morning after?\n\nPatient: Not really beyond the morning after, no. I mean, it definitely bothers me, and I don’t want to make it sound like I just shrug it off, because I don’t. The next morning I usually feel sluggish, kind of slow and heavy, and there’s this disappointed feeling in the background, like, Alejandro, you said you weren’t going to do that again, and then you did.\n\nBut I still function. I still get up and do the things I’m supposed to do. If I have errands, I go do them. If I have an appointment, I keep it. Around the house, I still help with what needs doing. It’s not like I’m unable to manage the day or I cancel everything because of it.\n\nIt’s more that I’m going through the normal routine feeling a little off, and not very proud of how I handled myself the night before. By later on, I’m usually moving along okay. So yes, it affects my mood some the next morning, but I wouldn’t say it carries on and stops me from functioning normally after that.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night and you keep going even though you meant to stop, does it feel like you're completely unable to control what you're doing, like you're losing control over your actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “completely unable” is a strong way to put it, but it does feel like something close to that in the moment. It’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’m aware. I’ll be standing there at night, picking at the leftovers, and the sensible part of me is saying, That’s enough, Alejandro. Close the container, put it away, go sit down.\n\nBut then my hand just keeps going back. One more bite, then another little piece, then I’m telling myself, Well, I already started, or I’ll stop after this. And I don’t stop when I meant to. That’s the part that bothers me, because it feels like my mind has made the decision to stop, but my body is still reaching for it anyway.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m blacked out or anything like that. I know it’s happening. But the control feels… weakened, I guess. Like I’m watching myself ignore the better judgment I already had."
